Even on shift days, when I know I’m burning way more than that. I keep telling myself it’s discipline, but I think it’s gone past that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess so. My turnout gear and even my station clothes hang a lot looser now. At first I kind of liked it, honestly—felt like I was getting sharper, more squared away.\n\nBut then during the fitness review they flagged my weight as too low for my build, which was… awkward. I remember thinking the scale at the station is always a little weird, because guys are always joking about it after lift days and stuff, but… yeah. Even after they said that, there’s still this part of me that thinks I could trim down a little more.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time, feeling like you couldn't stop?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so? Like, not in the way you’re describing, I guess.\n\nThere are times at the station where there’s a big meal laid out and everyone’s just piling food onto plates, and it kind of feels like this whole social thing—like if you don’t eat, people notice. But I wouldn’t say I’m eating a huge amount and can’t stop. I’m usually more… thinking about how much is on the plate, honestly.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going sideways. What do you mean by “large amount,” exactly?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I am. I don’t really like saying that, but yeah.\n\nLike if we have a big station meal, or even if I know I drank a bunch of water, and the scale is up even a pound, I get this tight feeling in my chest, like I’ve messed everything up. Like I blew it somehow.\n\nAnd it sounds stupid because I know weight jumps around, the guys talk about that all the time after workouts and stuff, but in my head it turns into… people are going to notice, they’re going to think I’m getting soft, or that I don’t have control. Then I start thinking I need to fix it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t know if I’d call it “loss of control,” exactly. Like… I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m eating, maybe too aware.

Sometimes at the station dinner table everything’s loud and fast and someone’s telling a story about a call or messing with the probie, and food is just there, and I’ll realize I took a few more bites than I meant to. But it’s not like I black out or can’t stop myself. It’s more like I get distracted for a second and then I’m immediately thinking, okay, how much was that?

So… no, I don’t think I’ve felt out of control eating. Not in the way that sounds.

I mean… I’ve thought it was excessive, yeah, but I don’t know if it would look that way to someone else.

In a short period of time… I’m not sure. I mean, maybe? Like sometimes after a long shift, especially if we’ve been running calls back to back, everyone just kind of eats fast because you don’t know when the tones are gonna drop again.

But I don’t know if that counts as excessive. I’ll think it is if I ate faster than I meant to, or if I didn’t really measure it in my head first. Like the station meals are weird that way—someone makes pasta or chili or whatever, and everybody’s piling food on plates and talking, and I’m over here trying to look normal but also keep track.

So… I guess I’ve felt like it was too much in the moment, but I couldn’t tell you if it was actually a lot of food in a short time. I don’t really know.

Like… I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m eating, maybe too aware.\n\nSometimes at the station dinner table everything’s loud and fast and someone’s telling a story about a call or messing with the probie, and food is just there, and I’ll realize I took a few more bites than I meant to. But it’s not like I black out or can’t stop myself. It’s more like I get distracted for a second and then I’m immediately thinking, okay, how much was that?\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve felt out of control eating. Not in the way that sounds.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’ve thought it was excessive, yeah, but I don’t know if it would look that way to someone else.\n\nLike if I have a normal plate at the station, sometimes in my head it feels like way too much, especially if everyone’s joking around and I’m not really tracking it perfectly. But then I’ll look at what other guys are eating and it’s like… mine isn’t even that much. So I don’t know. I guess I’ve considered it excessive, but I’m not sure that it actually was.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: In a short period of time… I’m not sure. I mean, maybe? Like sometimes after a long shift, especially if we’ve been running calls back to back, everyone just kind of eats fast because you don’t know when the tones are gonna drop again.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as excessive. I’ll think it is if I ate faster than I meant to, or if I didn’t really measure it in my head first. Like the station meals are weird that way—someone makes pasta or chili or whatever, and everybody’s piling food on plates and talking, and I’m over here trying to look normal but also keep track.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve felt like it was too much in the moment, but I couldn’t tell you if it was actually a lot of food in a short time. Guilty… I mean, yeah, maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if “guilty” is the exact word.

It’s more like I get annoyed with myself, like I should’ve paid more attention or I should’ve stopped sooner. But then again, at the station everybody’s eating fast, and if we just came back from a call, it’s kind of normal to just sit down and eat whatever’s there. One of the guys makes these huge breakfasts sometimes, like pancakes and eggs and the whole thing, and everyone acts like it’s this big team thing, you know?

So I don’t want to be weird about it. But afterward I’ll definitely think about it more than I probably should. Whether that’s guilt or just… I don’t know, being frustrated with myself, I’m not totally sure.

What do you mean by “large amount,” exactly?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I am. I don’t really like saying that, but yeah.\n\nLike if we have a big station meal, or even if I know I drank a bunch of water, and the scale is up even a pound, I get this tight feeling in my chest, like I’ve messed everything up. Like I blew it somehow.\n\nAnd it sounds stupid because I know weight jumps around, the guys talk about that all the time after workouts and stuff, but in my head it turns into… people are going to notice, they’re going to think I’m getting soft, or that I don’t have control. Then I start thinking I need to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a loss of control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “loss of control,” exactly. Like… I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m eating, maybe too aware.\n\nSometimes at the station dinner table everything’s loud and fast and someone’s telling a story about a call or messing with the probie, and food is just there, and I’ll realize I took a few more bites than I meant to. But it’s not like I black out or can’t stop myself. It’s more like I get distracted for a second and then I’m immediately thinking, okay, how much was that?\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve felt out of control eating. Not in the way that sounds.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’ve thought it was excessive, yeah, but I don’t know if it would look that way to someone else.\n\nLike if I have a normal plate at the station, sometimes in my head it feels like way too much, especially if everyone’s joking around and I’m not really tracking it perfectly. But then I’ll look at what other guys are eating and it’s like… mine isn’t even that much. So I don’t know. I guess I’ve considered it excessive, but I’m not sure that it actually was.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: In a short period of time… I’m not sure. I mean, maybe? Like sometimes after a long shift, especially if we’ve been running calls back to back, everyone just kind of eats fast because you don’t know when the tones are gonna drop again.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as excessive. I’ll think it is if I ate faster than I meant to, or if I didn’t really measure it in my head first. Like the station meals are weird that way—someone makes pasta or chili or whatever, and everybody’s piling food on plates and talking, and I’m over here trying to look normal but also keep track.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve felt like it was too much in the moment, but I couldn’t tell you if it was actually a lot of food in a short time. I don’t really know.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt guilty after eating?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I mean, yeah, maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if “guilty” is the exact word.\n\nIt’s more like I get annoyed with myself, like I should’ve paid more attention or I should’ve stopped sooner. But then again, at the station everybody’s eating fast, and if we just came back from a call, it’s kind of normal to just sit down and eat whatever’s there. One of the guys makes these huge breakfasts sometimes, like pancakes and eggs and the whole thing, and everyone acts like it’s this big team thing, you know?\n\nSo I don’t want to be weird about it. But afterward I’ll definitely think about it more than I probably should. Whether that’s guilt or just… I don’t know, being frustrated with myself, I’m not totally sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t know… I mean, I exercise because of the job, you know? Like we have to stay in shape, and there’s always workouts at the station or guys talking about some new routine. So… I guess I don’t have a clear answer. I don’t really know if it counts.

I mean… specifically? I don’t know. I don’t usually think, like, “I ate that, now I have to burn it off.” It’s more just this uncomfortable feeling sometimes, like I should be doing something instead of sitting there.

But then with the job, it all kind of blurs together. We’re always moving, or someone’s starting a workout, or there’s a drill, or the truck needs restocking. And at the station, if one person starts lifting, then suddenly three other guys are in there too, and it turns into this whole thing. So it’s hard for me to separate what’s just normal firefighter stuff from what’s… me overthinking food, I guess.

So I don’t have a clean yes-or-no answer. I haven’t really tracked it that way.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1, F50.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess I’m here because my eating’s gotten kind of out of hand. It started as me trying to eat cleaner, you know, for the lieutenant track—there’s pressure to be squared away, and at the station everyone’s got their routines and opinions about meal prep and all that.\n\nBut lately it’s become… like, I’ll just have black coffee, maybe a protein shake, and then a small dinner. Even on shift days, when I know I’m burning way more than that. I keep telling myself it’s discipline, but I think it’s gone past that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess so. My turnout gear and even my station clothes hang a lot looser now. At first I kind of liked it, honestly—felt like I was getting sharper, more squared away.\n\nBut then during the fitness review they flagged my weight as too low for my build, which was… awkward. I remember thinking the scale at the station is always a little weird, because guys are always joking about it after lift days and stuff, but… yeah. Even after they said that, there’s still this part of me that thinks I could trim down a little more.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time, feeling like you couldn't stop?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so? Like, not in the way you’re describing, I guess.\n\nThere are times at the station where there’s a big meal laid out and everyone’s just piling food onto plates, and it kind of feels like this whole social thing—like if you don’t eat, people notice. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1, F50.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess I’m here because my eating’s gotten kind of out of hand. It started as me trying to eat cleaner, you know, for the lieutenant track—there’s pressure to be squared away, and at the station everyone’s got their routines and opinions about meal prep and all that.\n\nBut lately it’s become… like, I’ll just have black coffee, maybe a protein shake, and then a small dinner. Even on shift days, when I know I’m burning way more than that. I keep telling myself it’s discipline, but I think it’s gone past that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess so. My turnout gear and even my station clothes hang a lot looser now. At first I kind of liked it, honestly—felt like I was getting sharper, more squared away.\n\nBut then during the fitness review they flagged my weight as too low for my build, which was… awkward. I remember thinking the scale at the station is always a little weird, because guys are always joking about it after lift days and stuff, but… yeah. Even after they said that, there’s still this part of me that thinks I could trim down a little more.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time, feeling like you couldn't stop?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so? Like, not in the way you’re describing, I guess.\n\nThere are times at the station where there’s a big meal laid out and everyone’s just piling food onto plates, and it kind of feels like this whole social thing—like if you don’t eat, people notice. But I wouldn’t say I’m eating a huge amount and can’t stop. I’m usually more… thinking about how much is on the plate, honestly.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going sideways. What do you mean by “large amount,” exactly?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I am. I don’t really like saying that, but yeah.\n\nLike if we have a big station meal, or even if I know I drank a bunch of water, and the scale is up even a pound, I get this tight feeling in my chest, like I’ve messed everything up. Like I blew it somehow.\n\nAnd it sounds stupid because I know weight jumps around, the guys talk about that all the time after workouts and stuff, but in my head it turns into… people are going to notice, they’re going to think I’m getting soft, or that I don’t have control. Then I start thinking I need to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a loss of control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “loss of control,” exactly. Like… I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m eating, maybe too aware.\n\nSometimes at the station dinner table everything’s loud and fast and someone’s telling a story about a call or messing with the probie, and food is just there, and I’ll realize I took a few more bites than I meant to. But it’s not like I black out or can’t stop myself. It’s more like I get distracted for a second and then I’m immediately thinking, okay, how much was that?\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve felt out of control eating. Not in the way that sounds.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’ve thought it was excessive, yeah, but I don’t know if it would look that way to someone else.\n\nLike if I have a normal plate at the station, sometimes in my head it feels like way too much, especially if everyone’s joking around and I’m not really tracking it perfectly. But then I’ll look at what other guys are eating and it’s like… mine isn’t even that much. So I don’t know. I guess I’ve considered it excessive, but I’m not sure that it actually was.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: In a short period of time… I’m not sure. I mean, maybe? Like sometimes after a long shift, especially if we’ve been running calls back to back, everyone just kind of eats fast because you don’t know when the tones are gonna drop again.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as excessive. I don’t really know.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt guilty after eating?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I mean, yeah, maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if “guilty” is the exact word.\n\nIt’s more like I get annoyed with myself, like I should’ve paid more attention or I should’ve stopped sooner. We’re hauling gear, checking equipment, doing drills, cleaning the bay—there’s always something.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t have a clear answer. I don’t really know if it counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise specifically to burn off calories or counteract something you've eaten?\n\nPatient: I mean… specifically? I don’t know. And at the station, if one person starts lifting, then suddenly three other guys are in there too, and it turns into this whole thing. I haven’t really tracked it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1, F50.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess I’m here because my eating’s gotten kind of out of hand. It started as me trying to eat cleaner, you know, for the lieutenant track—there’s pressure to be squared away, and at the station everyone’s got their routines and opinions about meal prep and all that.\n\nBut lately it’s become… like, I’ll just have black coffee, maybe a protein shake, and then a small dinner. Even on shift days, when I know I’m burning way more than that. I keep telling myself it’s discipline, but I think it’s gone past that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess so. My turnout gear and even my station clothes hang a lot looser now. At first I kind of liked it, honestly—felt like I was getting sharper, more squared away.\n\nBut then during the fitness review they flagged my weight as too low for my build, which was… awkward. I remember thinking the scale at the station is always a little weird, because guys are always joking about it after lift days and stuff, but… yeah. Even after they said that, there’s still this part of me that thinks I could trim down a little more.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time, feeling like you couldn't stop?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so? Like, not in the way you’re describing, I guess.\n\nThere are times at the station where there’s a big meal laid out and everyone’s just piling food onto plates, and it kind of feels like this whole social thing—like if you don’t eat, people notice. But I wouldn’t say I’m eating a huge amount and can’t stop. I’m usually more… thinking about how much is on the plate, honestly.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going sideways. What do you mean by “large amount,” exactly?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I am. I don’t really like saying that, but yeah.\n\nLike if we have a big station meal, or even if I know I drank a bunch of water, and the scale is up even a pound, I get this tight feeling in my chest, like I’ve messed everything up. Like I blew it somehow.\n\nAnd it sounds stupid because I know weight jumps around, the guys talk about that all the time after workouts and stuff, but in my head it turns into… people are going to notice, they’re going to think I’m getting soft, or that I don’t have control. Then I start thinking I need to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a loss of control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “loss of control,” exactly. Like… I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m eating, maybe too aware.\n\nSometimes at the station dinner table everything’s loud and fast and someone’s telling a story about a call or messing with the probie, and food is just there, and I’ll realize I took a few more bites than I meant to. But it’s not like I black out or can’t stop myself. It’s more like I get distracted for a second and then I’m immediately thinking, okay, how much was that?\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve felt out of control eating. Not in the way that sounds.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’ve thought it was excessive, yeah, but I don’t know if it would look that way to someone else.\n\nLike if I have a normal plate at the station, sometimes in my head it feels like way too much, especially if everyone’s joking around and I’m not really tracking it perfectly. But then I’ll look at what other guys are eating and it’s like… mine isn’t even that much. So I don’t know. I guess I’ve considered it excessive, but I’m not sure that it actually was.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: In a short period of time… I’m not sure. I mean, maybe? Like sometimes after a long shift, especially if we’ve been running calls back to back, everyone just kind of eats fast because you don’t know when the tones are gonna drop again.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as excessive. I’ll think it is if I ate faster than I meant to, or if I didn’t really measure it in my head first. Like the station meals are weird that way—someone makes pasta or chili or whatever, and everybody’s piling food on plates and talking, and I’m over here trying to look normal but also keep track.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve felt like it was too much in the moment, but I couldn’t tell you if it was actually a lot of food in a short time. I don’t really know.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt guilty after eating?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I mean, yeah, maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if “guilty” is the exact word.\n\nIt’s more like I get annoyed with myself, like I should’ve paid more attention or I should’ve stopped sooner. But then again, at the station everybody’s eating fast, and if we just came back from a call, it’s kind of normal to just sit down and eat whatever’s there. One of the guys makes these huge breakfasts sometimes, like pancakes and eggs and the whole thing, and everyone acts like it’s this big team thing, you know?\n\nSo I don’t want to be weird about it. But afterward I’ll definitely think about it more than I probably should. Whether that’s guilt or just… I don’t know, being frustrated with myself, I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to burn off calories or undo the effects of eating through exercise or other means?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I exercise because of the job, you know? Like we have to stay in shape, and there’s always workouts at the station or guys talking about some new routine. One of the captains is really into those circuit things, and if you don’t join in it’s kind of obvious, so sometimes I’ll just do it.\n\nBut as far as, like, specifically trying to undo eating… I’m not sure I think of it that way. Sometimes if I ate more than I meant to, I’ll feel like I should move around or not just sit there, but then that’s also just kind of normal at work. We’re hauling gear, checking equipment, doing drills, cleaning the bay—there’s always something.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t have a clear answer. I don’t really know if it counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise specifically to burn off calories or counteract something you've eaten?\n\nPatient: I mean… specifically? I don’t know. Whether that’s guilt or just… I don’t know, being frustrated with myself, I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to burn off calories or undo the effects of eating through exercise or other means?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I exercise because of the job, you know? Like we have to stay in shape, and there’s always workouts at the station or guys talking about some new routine. One of the captains is really into those circuit things, and if you don’t join in it’s kind of obvious, so sometimes I’ll just do it.\n\nBut as far as, like, specifically trying to undo eating… I’m not sure I think of it that way. Sometimes if I ate more than I meant to, I’ll feel like I should move around or not just sit there, but then that’s also just kind of normal at work. We’re hauling gear, checking equipment, doing drills, cleaning the bay—there’s always something.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t have a clear answer. I don’t really know if it counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise specifically to burn off calories or counteract something you've eaten?\n\nPatient: I mean… specifically? I don’t know. I don’t usually think, like, “I ate that, now I have to burn it off.” It’s more just this uncomfortable feeling sometimes, like I should be doing something instead of sitting there.\n\nBut then with the job, it all kind of blurs together. We’re always moving, or someone’s starting a workout, or there’s a drill, or the truck needs restocking. And at the station, if one person starts lifting, then suddenly three other guys are in there too, and it turns into this whole thing. So it’s hard for me to separate what’s just normal firefighter stuff from what’s… me overthinking food, I guess.\n\nSo I don’t have a clean yes-or-no answer. I haven’t really tracked it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve thrown up before like anyone has, if I’m sick or something, or after one of those awful stomach bugs that goes around the station because everyone’s sharing the same kitchen and nobody wipes anything down like they should.\n\nBut making myself do it after eating? That’s not really something I do. I wouldn’t even know… I mean, I guess people do that, but no, that’s not something I’ve been doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics after eating?\n\nPatient: Uh… laxatives like, for constipation? I mean, I don’t really… I’m not sure. We’ve got random stuff at the station, like guys leave antacids and allergy pills and whatever in the cabinet, and half the time nobody knows whose it is, which probably isn’t great.\n\nBut after eating, specifically? No, I don’t think that’s something I do. Diuretics… are those like water pills? I don’t take anything like that after meals. I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way, but no, not really.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… probably. I mean, not like I’m trying to look like some model or anything, it’s more… at the station there’s just this expectation that you look squared away. Like your gear’s clean, your boots are right, you don’t look sloppy.

And before work, I’ll catch myself in the mirror—like in the bathroom before I leave—and I’m checking if my waist looks… I don’t know, tight enough, or if my face looks kind of sharp instead of puffy. Which sounds stupid saying out loud. But if I don’t think I look right, it can kind of throw me off. Like I don’t feel as confident walking in, or like I don’t look as ready to be in charge of things.

And then, I don’t know, the guys notice everything. Not in a mean way always, just station culture. Somebody gets a haircut and it’s a whole discussion for twenty minutes. So yeah, I think how I look probably affects me more than I want to admit.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess I’m here because my eating’s gotten kind of out of hand. It started as me trying to eat cleaner, you know, for the lieutenant track—there’s pressure to be squared away, and at the station everyone’s got their routines and opinions about meal prep and all that.\n\nBut lately it’s become… like, I’ll just have black coffee, maybe a protein shake, and then a small dinner. Even on shift days, when I know I’m burning way more than that. I keep telling myself it’s discipline, but I think it’s gone past that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess so. My turnout gear and even my station clothes hang a lot looser now. At first I kind of liked it, honestly—felt like I was getting sharper, more squared away.\n\nBut then during the fitness review they flagged my weight as too low for my build, which was… awkward. I remember thinking the scale at the station is always a little weird, because guys are always joking about it after lift days and stuff, but… yeah. Even after they said that, there’s still this part of me that thinks I could trim down a little more.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time, feeling like you couldn't stop?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so? Like, not in the way you’re describing, I guess.\n\nThere are times at the station where there’s a big meal laid out and everyone’s just piling food onto plates, and it kind of feels like this whole social thing—like if you don’t eat, people notice. But I wouldn’t say I’m eating a huge amount and can’t stop. I’m usually more… thinking about how much is on the plate, honestly.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going sideways. What do you mean by “large amount,” exactly?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I am. I don’t really like saying that, but yeah.\n\nLike if we have a big station meal, or even if I know I drank a bunch of water, and the scale is up even a pound, I get this tight feeling in my chest, like I’ve messed everything up. Like I blew it somehow.\n\nAnd it sounds stupid because I know weight jumps around, the guys talk about that all the time after workouts and stuff, but in my head it turns into… people are going to notice, they’re going to think I’m getting soft, or that I don’t have control. Then I start thinking I need to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a loss of control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “loss of control,” exactly. Like… I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m eating, maybe too aware.\n\nSometimes at the station dinner table everything’s loud and fast and someone’s telling a story about a call or messing with the probie, and food is just there, and I’ll realize I took a few more bites than I meant to. But it’s not like I black out or can’t stop myself. It’s more like I get distracted for a second and then I’m immediately thinking, okay, how much was that?\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve felt out of control eating. Not in the way that sounds.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’ve thought it was excessive, yeah, but I don’t know if it would look that way to someone else.\n\nLike if I have a normal plate at the station, sometimes in my head it feels like way too much, especially if everyone’s joking around and I’m not really tracking it perfectly. But then I’ll look at what other guys are eating and it’s like… mine isn’t even that much. So I don’t know. I guess I’ve considered it excessive, but I’m not sure that it actually was.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: In a short period of time… I’m not sure. I mean, maybe? Like sometimes after a long shift, especially if we’ve been running calls back to back, everyone just kind of eats fast because you don’t know when the tones are gonna drop again.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as excessive. I’ll think it is if I ate faster than I meant to, or if I didn’t really measure it in my head first. Like the station meals are weird that way—someone makes pasta or chili or whatever, and everybody’s piling food on plates and talking, and I’m over here trying to look normal but also keep track.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve felt like it was too much in the moment, but I couldn’t tell you if it was actually a lot of food in a short time. I don’t really know.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt guilty after eating?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I mean, yeah, maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if “guilty” is the exact word.\n\nIt’s more like I get annoyed with myself, like I should’ve paid more attention or I should’ve stopped sooner. But then again, at the station everybody’s eating fast, and if we just came back from a call, it’s kind of normal to just sit down and eat whatever’s there. One of the guys makes these huge breakfasts sometimes, like pancakes and eggs and the whole thing, and everyone acts like it’s this big team thing, you know?\n\nSo I don’t want to be weird about it. But afterward I’ll definitely think about it more than I probably should. Whether that’s guilt or just… I don’t know, being frustrated with myself, I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to burn off calories or undo the effects of eating through exercise or other means?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I exercise because of the job, you know? Like we have to stay in shape, and there’s always workouts at the station or guys talking about some new routine. One of the captains is really into those circuit things, and if you don’t join in it’s kind of obvious, so sometimes I’ll just do it.\n\nBut as far as, like, specifically trying to undo eating… I’m not sure I think of it that way. Sometimes if I ate more than I meant to, I’ll feel like I should move around or not just sit there, but then that’s also just kind of normal at work. We’re hauling gear, checking equipment, doing drills, cleaning the bay—there’s always something.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t have a clear answer. I don’t really know if it counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise specifically to burn off calories or counteract something you've eaten?\n\nPatient: I mean… specifically? I don’t know. No, I don’t think that’s something I do. Diuretics… are those like water pills? I don’t take anything like that after meals. I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way, but no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are influenced by how you want to look or be perceived by others at the station?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. But it’s not like I black out or can’t stop myself. It’s more like I get distracted for a second and then I’m immediately thinking, okay, how much was that?\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve felt out of control eating. Not in the way that sounds.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’ve thought it was excessive, yeah, but I don’t know if it would look that way to someone else.\n\nLike if I have a normal plate at the station, sometimes in my head it feels like way too much, especially if everyone’s joking around and I’m not really tracking it perfectly. But then I’ll look at what other guys are eating and it’s like… mine isn’t even that much. So I don’t know. I guess I’ve considered it excessive, but I’m not sure that it actually was.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: In a short period of time… I’m not sure. I mean, maybe? Like sometimes after a long shift, especially if we’ve been running calls back to back, everyone just kind of eats fast because you don’t know when the tones are gonna drop again.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as excessive. I’ll think it is if I ate faster than I meant to, or if I didn’t really measure it in my head first. Like the station meals are weird that way—someone makes pasta or chili or whatever, and everybody’s piling food on plates and talking, and I’m over here trying to look normal but also keep track.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve felt like it was too much in the moment, but I couldn’t tell you if it was actually a lot of food in a short time. I don’t really know.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt guilty after eating?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I mean, yeah, maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if “guilty” is the exact word.\n\nIt’s more like I get annoyed with myself, like I should’ve paid more attention or I should’ve stopped sooner. But then again, at the station everybody’s eating fast, and if we just came back from a call, it’s kind of normal to just sit down and eat whatever’s there. One of the guys makes these huge breakfasts sometimes, like pancakes and eggs and the whole thing, and everyone acts like it’s this big team thing, you know?\n\nSo I don’t want to be weird about it. But afterward I’ll definitely think about it more than I probably should. Whether that’s guilt or just… I don’t know, being frustrated with myself, I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to burn off calories or undo the effects of eating through exercise or other means?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I exercise because of the job, you know? Like we have to stay in shape, and there’s always workouts at the station or guys talking about some new routine. One of the captains is really into those circuit things, and if you don’t join in it’s kind of obvious, so sometimes I’ll just do it.\n\nBut as far as, like, specifically trying to undo eating… I’m not sure I think of it that way. Sometimes if I ate more than I meant to, I’ll feel like I should move around or not just sit there, but then that’s also just kind of normal at work. We’re hauling gear, checking equipment, doing drills, cleaning the bay—there’s always something.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t have a clear answer. I don’t really know if it counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise specifically to burn off calories or counteract something you've eaten?\n\nPatient: I mean… specifically? I don’t know. I don’t usually think, like, “I ate that, now I have to burn it off.” It’s more just this uncomfortable feeling sometimes, like I should be doing something instead of sitting there.\n\nBut then with the job, it all kind of blurs together. We’re always moving, or someone’s starting a workout, or there’s a drill, or the truck needs restocking. And at the station, if one person starts lifting, then suddenly three other guys are in there too, and it turns into this whole thing. So it’s hard for me to separate what’s just normal firefighter stuff from what’s… me overthinking food, I guess.\n\nSo I don’t have a clean yes-or-no answer. I haven’t really tracked it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve thrown up before like anyone has, if I’m sick or something, or after one of those awful stomach bugs that goes around the station because everyone’s sharing the same kitchen and nobody wipes anything down like they should.\n\nBut making myself do it after eating? That’s not really something I do. I wouldn’t even know… I mean, I guess people do that, but no, that’s not something I’ve been doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics after eating?\n\nPatient: Uh… laxatives like, for constipation? I mean, I don’t really… I’m not sure. We’ve got random stuff at the station, like guys leave antacids and allergy pills and whatever in the cabinet, and half the time nobody knows whose it is, which probably isn’t great.\n\nBut after eating, specifically? No, I don’t think that’s something I do. Diuretics… are those like water pills? I don’t take anything like that after meals. I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way, but no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are influenced by how you want to look or be perceived by others at the station?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. I mean, not like I’m trying to look like some model or anything, it’s more… at the station there’s just this expectation that you look squared away. Like your gear’s clean, your boots are right, you don’t look sloppy.\n\nAnd before work, I’ll catch myself in the mirror—like in the bathroom before I leave—and I’m checking if my waist looks… I don’t know, tight enough, or if my face looks kind of sharp instead of puffy. Which sounds stupid saying out loud. But if I don’t think I look right, it can kind of throw me off. Like I don’t feel as confident walking in, or like I don’t look as ready to be in charge of things.\n\nAnd then, I don’t know, the guys notice everything. Not in a mean way always, just station culture. Somebody gets a haircut and it’s a whole discussion for twenty minutes. So yeah, I think how I look probably affects me more than I want to admit.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I mean… I’m still doing the job. Calls come in, I’m there, I do what I’m supposed to do. It’s not like I’m missing shifts or anything.

One of the guys makes these huge breakfasts sometimes, like pancakes and eggs and the whole thing, and everyone acts like it’s this big team thing, you know?\n\nSo I don’t want to be weird about it. But afterward I’ll definitely think about it more than I probably should. Whether that’s guilt or just… I don’t know, being frustrated with myself, I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to burn off calories or undo the effects of eating through exercise or other means?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I exercise because of the job, you know? Like we have to stay in shape, and there’s always workouts at the station or guys talking about some new routine. One of the captains is really into those circuit things, and if you don’t join in it’s kind of obvious, so sometimes I’ll just do it.\n\nBut as far as, like, specifically trying to undo eating… I’m not sure I think of it that way. Sometimes if I ate more than I meant to, I’ll feel like I should move around or not just sit there, but then that’s also just kind of normal at work. I don’t know if “happy” is the right word. Like, if I wasn’t a firefighter… maybe I wouldn’t think about it as much? Because a lot of it happens right before work, honestly.

I’ll be getting ready and I’ll look in the mirror, and I’m not even thinking about a number exactly. So yeah, the job probably turns the volume up on it. But I can’t say for sure I’d just be fine with my weight if I wasn’t doing this.

I mean, maybe? Like sometimes after a long shift, especially if we’ve been running calls back to back, everyone just kind of eats fast because you don’t know when the tones are gonna drop again.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as excessive. I’ll think it is if I ate faster than I meant to, or if I didn’t really measure it in my head first. Like the station meals are weird that way—someone makes pasta or chili or whatever, and everybody’s piling food on plates and talking, and I’m over here trying to look normal but also keep track.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve felt like it was too much in the moment, but I couldn’t tell you if it was actually a lot of food in a short time. I don’t really know.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt guilty after eating?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I mean, yeah, maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if “guilty” is the exact word.\n\nIt’s more like I get annoyed with myself, like I should’ve paid more attention or I should’ve stopped sooner. But then again, at the station everybody’s eating fast, and if we just came back from a call, it’s kind of normal to just sit down and eat whatever’s there. One of the guys makes these huge breakfasts sometimes, like pancakes and eggs and the whole thing, and everyone acts like it’s this big team thing, you know?\n\nSo I don’t want to be weird about it. But afterward I’ll definitely think about it more than I probably should. Whether that’s guilt or just… I don’t know, being frustrated with myself, I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to burn off calories or undo the effects of eating through exercise or other means?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I exercise because of the job, you know? Like we have to stay in shape, and there’s always workouts at the station or guys talking about some new routine. One of the captains is really into those circuit things, and if you don’t join in it’s kind of obvious, so sometimes I’ll just do it.\n\nBut as far as, like, specifically trying to undo eating… I’m not sure I think of it that way. Sometimes if I ate more than I meant to, I’ll feel like I should move around or not just sit there, but then that’s also just kind of normal at work. We’re hauling gear, checking equipment, doing drills, cleaning the bay—there’s always something.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t have a clear answer. I don’t really know if it counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise specifically to burn off calories or counteract something you've eaten?\n\nPatient: I mean… specifically? I don’t know. I don’t usually think, like, “I ate that, now I have to burn it off.” It’s more just this uncomfortable feeling sometimes, like I should be doing something instead of sitting there.\n\nBut then with the job, it all kind of blurs together. We’re always moving, or someone’s starting a workout, or there’s a drill, or the truck needs restocking. And at the station, if one person starts lifting, then suddenly three other guys are in there too, and it turns into this whole thing. So it’s hard for me to separate what’s just normal firefighter stuff from what’s… me overthinking food, I guess.\n\nSo I don’t have a clean yes-or-no answer. I haven’t really tracked it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve thrown up before like anyone has, if I’m sick or something, or after one of those awful stomach bugs that goes around the station because everyone’s sharing the same kitchen and nobody wipes anything down like they should.\n\nBut making myself do it after eating? That’s not really something I do. I wouldn’t even know… I mean, I guess people do that, but no, that’s not something I’ve been doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics after eating?\n\nPatient: Uh… laxatives like, for constipation? I mean, I don’t really… I’m not sure. We’ve got random stuff at the station, like guys leave antacids and allergy pills and whatever in the cabinet, and half the time nobody knows whose it is, which probably isn’t great.\n\nBut after eating, specifically? No, I don’t think that’s something I do. Diuretics… are those like water pills? I don’t take anything like that after meals. I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way, but no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are influenced by how you want to look or be perceived by others at the station?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. I mean, not like I’m trying to look like some model or anything, it’s more… at the station there’s just this expectation that you look squared away. Like your gear’s clean, your boots are right, you don’t look sloppy.\n\nAnd before work, I’ll catch myself in the mirror—like in the bathroom before I leave—and I’m checking if my waist looks… I don’t know, tight enough, or if my face looks kind of sharp instead of puffy. Which sounds stupid saying out loud. But if I don’t think I look right, it can kind of throw me off. Like I don’t feel as confident walking in, or like I don’t look as ready to be in charge of things.\n\nAnd then, I don’t know, the guys notice everything. Not in a mean way always, just station culture. Somebody gets a haircut and it’s a whole discussion for twenty minutes. So yeah, I think how I look probably affects me more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your eating habits are interfering with your ability to perform your job as a firefighter?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still doing the job. Calls come in, I’m there, I do what I’m supposed to do. It’s not like I’m missing shifts or anything.\n\nBut… yeah, maybe in the station side of things it’s changed. Like when the crew orders food or everybody sits down for a meal, I’ve started kind of finding reasons to be somewhere else. Checking gear, cleaning something, saying I already ate. And station meals are a big part of being on the crew, you know? Half the bonding happens over takeout or whatever somebody made.\n\nSo I guess I’m keeping to myself more than I used to. I don’t know if that counts as affecting the job exactly, but I’m definitely less… part of the team lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be happy with your weight if you weren't a firefighter?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “happy” is the right word. Like, if I wasn’t a firefighter… maybe I wouldn’t think about it as much? Because a lot of it happens right before work, honestly.\n\nI’ll be getting ready and I’ll look in the mirror, and I’m not even thinking about a number exactly. I’m looking at my waist, or my face, like… do I look sharp enough, do I look kind of puffy, do I look ready. And if I don’t like what I see, it throws me off. I feel less confident walking into the station, like I don’t look as command-ready or squared away.\n\nSo yeah, the job probably turns the volume up on it. But I can’t say for sure I’d just be fine with my weight if I wasn’t doing this.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

But I can’t say for sure I’d just be fine with my weight if I wasn’t doing this.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre you trying to reach a specific weight or body shape?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 27 / firefighter / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance\nmanifestation: \"When I look in the mirror before work, I focus on whether my waist and face look sharp enough, and if they don’t, it messes with how confident and command-ready I feel that day.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not like a specific number, no. I don’t have some exact weight written down or anything.

It’s more… I get stuck on how I look right before work. Like I’ll be in the mirror checking if my waist looks tight enough, or if my face looks kind of sharp, not puffy. And if it doesn’t look right to me, it messes with my head going in. I feel less confident, like I don’t look as… ready to be in charge, I guess.

Which is weird, because with firefighting it’s not supposed to be about that. It’s supposed to be whether you can do the job, whether your gear’s right, whether you’re squared away. But somehow that “squared away” thing turns into me looking at my body too much.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess I’m here because my eating’s gotten kind of out of hand. It started as me trying to eat cleaner, you know, for the lieutenant track—there’s pressure to be squared away, and at the station everyone’s got their routines and opinions about meal prep and all that.\n\nBut lately it’s become… like, I’ll just have black coffee, maybe a protein shake, and then a small dinner. Even on shift days, when I know I’m burning way more than that. I keep telling myself it’s discipline, but I think it’s gone past that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess so. My turnout gear and even my station clothes hang a lot looser now. At first I kind of liked it, honestly—felt like I was getting sharper, more squared away.\n\nBut then during the fitness review they flagged my weight as too low for my build, which was… awkward. I remember thinking the scale at the station is always a little weird, because guys are always joking about it after lift days and stuff, but… yeah. Even after they said that, there’s still this part of me that thinks I could trim down a little more.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time, feeling like you couldn't stop?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so? Like, not in the way you’re describing, I guess.\n\nThere are times at the station where there’s a big meal laid out and everyone’s just piling food onto plates, and it kind of feels like this whole social thing—like if you don’t eat, people notice. But I wouldn’t say I’m eating a huge amount and can’t stop. I’m usually more… thinking about how much is on the plate, honestly.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going sideways. What do you mean by “large amount,” exactly?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I am. I don’t really like saying that, but yeah.\n\nLike if we have a big station meal, or even if I know I drank a bunch of water, and the scale is up even a pound, I get this tight feeling in my chest, like I’ve messed everything up. Like I blew it somehow.\n\nAnd it sounds stupid because I know weight jumps around, the guys talk about that all the time after workouts and stuff, but in my head it turns into… people are going to notice, they’re going to think I’m getting soft, or that I don’t have control. Then I start thinking I need to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a loss of control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “loss of control,” exactly. Like… I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m eating, maybe too aware.\n\nSometimes at the station dinner table everything’s loud and fast and someone’s telling a story about a call or messing with the probie, and food is just there, and I’ll realize I took a few more bites than I meant to. But it’s not like I black out or can’t stop myself. It’s more like I get distracted for a second and then I’m immediately thinking, okay, how much was that?\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve felt out of control eating. Not in the way that sounds.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’ve thought it was excessive, yeah, but I don’t know if it would look that way to someone else.\n\nLike if I have a normal plate at the station, sometimes in my head it feels like way too much, especially if everyone’s joking around and I’m not really tracking it perfectly. But then I’ll look at what other guys are eating and it’s like… mine isn’t even that much. So I don’t know. I guess I’ve considered it excessive, but I’m not sure that it actually was.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: In a short period of time… I’m not sure. I mean, maybe? Like sometimes after a long shift, especially if we’ve been running calls back to back, everyone just kind of eats fast because you don’t know when the tones are gonna drop again.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as excessive. I’ll think it is if I ate faster than I meant to, or if I didn’t really measure it in my head first. Like the station meals are weird that way—someone makes pasta or chili or whatever, and everybody’s piling food on plates and talking, and I’m over here trying to look normal but also keep track.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve felt like it was too much in the moment, but I couldn’t tell you if it was actually a lot of food in a short time. I don’t really know.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt guilty after eating?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I mean, yeah, maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if “guilty” is the exact word.\n\nIt’s more like I get annoyed with myself, like I should’ve paid more attention or I should’ve stopped sooner. But then again, at the station everybody’s eating fast, and if we just came back from a call, it’s kind of normal to just sit down and eat whatever’s there. One of the guys makes these huge breakfasts sometimes, like pancakes and eggs and the whole thing, and everyone acts like it’s this big team thing, you know?\n\nSo I don’t want to be weird about it. But afterward I’ll definitely think about it more than I probably should. Whether that’s guilt or just… I don’t know, being frustrated with myself, I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to burn off calories or undo the effects of eating through exercise or other means?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I exercise because of the job, you know? Like we have to stay in shape, and there’s always workouts at the station or guys talking about some new routine. One of the captains is really into those circuit things, and if you don’t join in it’s kind of obvious, so sometimes I’ll just do it.\n\nBut as far as, like, specifically trying to undo eating… I’m not sure I think of it that way. Sometimes if I ate more than I meant to, I’ll feel like I should move around or not just sit there, but then that’s also just kind of normal at work. We’re hauling gear, checking equipment, doing drills, cleaning the bay—there’s always something.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t have a clear answer. I don’t really know if it counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise specifically to burn off calories or counteract something you've eaten?\n\nPatient: I mean… specifically? I don’t know. I don’t usually think, like, “I ate that, now I have to burn it off.” It’s more just this uncomfortable feeling sometimes, like I should be doing something instead of sitting there.\n\nBut then with the job, it all kind of blurs together. We’re always moving, or someone’s starting a workout, or there’s a drill, or the truck needs restocking. And at the station, if one person starts lifting, then suddenly three other guys are in there too, and it turns into this whole thing. So it’s hard for me to separate what’s just normal firefighter stuff from what’s… me overthinking food, I guess.\n\nSo I don’t have a clean yes-or-no answer. I haven’t really tracked it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve thrown up before like anyone has, if I’m sick or something, or after one of those awful stomach bugs that goes around the station because everyone’s sharing the same kitchen and nobody wipes anything down like they should.\n\nBut making myself do it after eating? That’s not really something I do. I wouldn’t even know… I mean, I guess people do that, but no, that’s not something I’ve been doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics after eating?\n\nPatient: Uh… laxatives like, for constipation? I mean, I don’t really… I’m not sure. We’ve got random stuff at the station, like guys leave antacids and allergy pills and whatever in the cabinet, and half the time nobody knows whose it is, which probably isn’t great.\n\nBut after eating, specifically? No, I don’t think that’s something I do. Diuretics… are those like water pills? I don’t take anything like that after meals. I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way, but no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are influenced by how you want to look or be perceived by others at the station?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. I mean, not like I’m trying to look like some model or anything, it’s more… at the station there’s just this expectation that you look squared away. Like your gear’s clean, your boots are right, you don’t look sloppy.\n\nAnd before work, I’ll catch myself in the mirror—like in the bathroom before I leave—and I’m checking if my waist looks… I don’t know, tight enough, or if my face looks kind of sharp instead of puffy. Which sounds stupid saying out loud. But if I don’t think I look right, it can kind of throw me off. Like I don’t feel as confident walking in, or like I don’t look as ready to be in charge of things.\n\nAnd then, I don’t know, the guys notice everything. Not in a mean way always, just station culture. Somebody gets a haircut and it’s a whole discussion for twenty minutes. So yeah, I think how I look probably affects me more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your eating habits are interfering with your ability to perform your job as a firefighter?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still doing the job. Calls come in, I’m there, I do what I’m supposed to do. It’s not like I’m missing shifts or anything.\n\nBut… yeah, maybe in the station side of things it’s changed. Like when the crew orders food or everybody sits down for a meal, I’ve started kind of finding reasons to be somewhere else. Checking gear, cleaning something, saying I already ate. And station meals are a big part of being on the crew, you know? Half the bonding happens over takeout or whatever somebody made.\n\nSo I guess I’m keeping to myself more than I used to. I don’t know if that counts as affecting the job exactly, but I’m definitely less… part of the team lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be happy with your weight if you weren't a firefighter?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “happy” is the right word. Like, if I wasn’t a firefighter… maybe I wouldn’t think about it as much? Because a lot of it happens right before work, honestly.\n\nI’ll be getting ready and I’ll look in the mirror, and I’m not even thinking about a number exactly. I’m looking at my waist, or my face, like… do I look sharp enough, do I look kind of puffy, do I look ready. And if I don’t like what I see, it throws me off. I feel less confident walking into the station, like I don’t look as command-ready or squared away.\n\nSo yeah, the job probably turns the volume up on it. But I can’t say for sure I’d just be fine with my weight if I wasn’t doing this.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to reach a specific weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: Not like a specific number, no. I don’t have some exact weight written down or anything.\n\nIt’s more… I get stuck on how I look right before work. I feel less confident, like I don’t look as… ready to be in charge, I guess.\n\nWhich is weird, because with firefighting it’s not supposed to be about that. It’s supposed to be whether you can do the job, whether your gear’s right, whether you’re squared away. But somehow that “squared away” thing turns into me looking at my body too much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d say “need” exactly… like nobody at the station is telling me, “You have to look a certain way.” It’s not like that.

But in my head, yeah, kind of. Before work I’ll catch myself in the mirror, and I’m looking at my waist, my face, whether I look sharp enough or kind of soft or puffy. And if it doesn’t look right, it messes with me. I feel less confident walking in, like I’m not as command-ready somehow.

Which sounds stupid saying out loud, because being a good firefighter is supposed to be about doing the work, not your face in a mirror. But I guess I do connect it in my head more than I should.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess I’m here because my eating’s gotten kind of out of hand. It started as me trying to eat cleaner, you know, for the lieutenant track—there’s pressure to be squared away, and at the station everyone’s got their routines and opinions about meal prep and all that.\n\nBut lately it’s become… like, I’ll just have black coffee, maybe a protein shake, and then a small dinner. Even on shift days, when I know I’m burning way more than that. I keep telling myself it’s discipline, but I think it’s gone past that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess so. My turnout gear and even my station clothes hang a lot looser now. At first I kind of liked it, honestly—felt like I was getting sharper, more squared away.\n\nBut then during the fitness review they flagged my weight as too low for my build, which was… awkward. I remember thinking the scale at the station is always a little weird, because guys are always joking about it after lift days and stuff, but… yeah. Even after they said that, there’s still this part of me that thinks I could trim down a little more.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time, feeling like you couldn't stop?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so? Like, not in the way you’re describing, I guess.\n\nThere are times at the station where there’s a big meal laid out and everyone’s just piling food onto plates, and it kind of feels like this whole social thing—like if you don’t eat, people notice. But I wouldn’t say I’m eating a huge amount and can’t stop. I’m usually more… thinking about how much is on the plate, honestly.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going sideways. What do you mean by “large amount,” exactly?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I am. I don’t really like saying that, but yeah.\n\nLike if we have a big station meal, or even if I know I drank a bunch of water, and the scale is up even a pound, I get this tight feeling in my chest, like I’ve messed everything up. Like I blew it somehow.\n\nAnd it sounds stupid because I know weight jumps around, the guys talk about that all the time after workouts and stuff, but in my head it turns into… people are going to notice, they’re going to think I’m getting soft, or that I don’t have control. Then I start thinking I need to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a loss of control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “loss of control,” exactly. Like… I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m eating, maybe too aware.\n\nSometimes at the station dinner table everything’s loud and fast and someone’s telling a story about a call or messing with the probie, and food is just there, and I’ll realize I took a few more bites than I meant to. But it’s not like I black out or can’t stop myself. It’s more like I get distracted for a second and then I’m immediately thinking, okay, how much was that?\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve felt out of control eating. Not in the way that sounds.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’ve thought it was excessive, yeah, but I don’t know if it would look that way to someone else.\n\nLike if I have a normal plate at the station, sometimes in my head it feels like way too much, especially if everyone’s joking around and I’m not really tracking it perfectly. But then I’ll look at what other guys are eating and it’s like… mine isn’t even that much. So I don’t know. I guess I’ve considered it excessive, but I’m not sure that it actually was.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: In a short period of time… I’m not sure. I mean, maybe? Like sometimes after a long shift, especially if we’ve been running calls back to back, everyone just kind of eats fast because you don’t know when the tones are gonna drop again.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as excessive. I’ll think it is if I ate faster than I meant to, or if I didn’t really measure it in my head first. Like the station meals are weird that way—someone makes pasta or chili or whatever, and everybody’s piling food on plates and talking, and I’m over here trying to look normal but also keep track.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve felt like it was too much in the moment, but I couldn’t tell you if it was actually a lot of food in a short time. I don’t really know.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt guilty after eating?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I mean, yeah, maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if “guilty” is the exact word.\n\nIt’s more like I get annoyed with myself, like I should’ve paid more attention or I should’ve stopped sooner. But then again, at the station everybody’s eating fast, and if we just came back from a call, it’s kind of normal to just sit down and eat whatever’s there. One of the guys makes these huge breakfasts sometimes, like pancakes and eggs and the whole thing, and everyone acts like it’s this big team thing, you know?\n\nSo I don’t want to be weird about it. But afterward I’ll definitely think about it more than I probably should. Whether that’s guilt or just… I don’t know, being frustrated with myself, I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to burn off calories or undo the effects of eating through exercise or other means?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I exercise because of the job, you know? Like we have to stay in shape, and there’s always workouts at the station or guys talking about some new routine. One of the captains is really into those circuit things, and if you don’t join in it’s kind of obvious, so sometimes I’ll just do it.\n\nBut as far as, like, specifically trying to undo eating… I’m not sure I think of it that way. Sometimes if I ate more than I meant to, I’ll feel like I should move around or not just sit there, but then that’s also just kind of normal at work. We’re hauling gear, checking equipment, doing drills, cleaning the bay—there’s always something.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t have a clear answer. I don’t really know if it counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise specifically to burn off calories or counteract something you've eaten?\n\nPatient: I mean… specifically? I don’t know. I don’t usually think, like, “I ate that, now I have to burn it off.” It’s more just this uncomfortable feeling sometimes, like I should be doing something instead of sitting there.\n\nBut then with the job, it all kind of blurs together. We’re always moving, or someone’s starting a workout, or there’s a drill, or the truck needs restocking. And at the station, if one person starts lifting, then suddenly three other guys are in there too, and it turns into this whole thing. So it’s hard for me to separate what’s just normal firefighter stuff from what’s… me overthinking food, I guess.\n\nSo I don’t have a clean yes-or-no answer. I haven’t really tracked it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve thrown up before like anyone has, if I’m sick or something, or after one of those awful stomach bugs that goes around the station because everyone’s sharing the same kitchen and nobody wipes anything down like they should.\n\nBut making myself do it after eating? That’s not really something I do. I wouldn’t even know… I mean, I guess people do that, but no, that’s not something I’ve been doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics after eating?\n\nPatient: Uh… laxatives like, for constipation? I mean, I don’t really… I’m not sure. We’ve got random stuff at the station, like guys leave antacids and allergy pills and whatever in the cabinet, and half the time nobody knows whose it is, which probably isn’t great.\n\nBut after eating, specifically? No, I don’t think that’s something I do. Diuretics… are those like water pills? I don’t take anything like that after meals. I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way, but no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are influenced by how you want to look or be perceived by others at the station?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. I mean, not like I’m trying to look like some model or anything, it’s more… at the station there’s just this expectation that you look squared away. Like your gear’s clean, your boots are right, you don’t look sloppy.\n\nAnd before work, I’ll catch myself in the mirror—like in the bathroom before I leave—and I’m checking if my waist looks… I don’t know, tight enough, or if my face looks kind of sharp instead of puffy. Which sounds stupid saying out loud. But if I don’t think I look right, it can kind of throw me off. Like I don’t feel as confident walking in, or like I don’t look as ready to be in charge of things.\n\nAnd then, I don’t know, the guys notice everything. Not in a mean way always, just station culture. Somebody gets a haircut and it’s a whole discussion for twenty minutes. So yeah, I think how I look probably affects me more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your eating habits are interfering with your ability to perform your job as a firefighter?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still doing the job. Calls come in, I’m there, I do what I’m supposed to do. It’s not like I’m missing shifts or anything.\n\nBut… yeah, maybe in the station side of things it’s changed. Like when the crew orders food or everybody sits down for a meal, I’ve started kind of finding reasons to be somewhere else. Checking gear, cleaning something, saying I already ate. And station meals are a big part of being on the crew, you know? Half the bonding happens over takeout or whatever somebody made.\n\nSo I guess I’m keeping to myself more than I used to. I don’t know if that counts as affecting the job exactly, but I’m definitely less… part of the team lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be happy with your weight if you weren't a firefighter?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “happy” is the right word. Like, if I wasn’t a firefighter… maybe I wouldn’t think about it as much? Because a lot of it happens right before work, honestly.\n\nI’ll be getting ready and I’ll look in the mirror, and I’m not even thinking about a number exactly. I’m looking at my waist, or my face, like… do I look sharp enough, do I look kind of puffy, do I look ready. And if I don’t like what I see, it throws me off. I feel less confident walking into the station, like I don’t look as command-ready or squared away.\n\nSo yeah, the job probably turns the volume up on it. But I can’t say for sure I’d just be fine with my weight if I wasn’t doing this.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to reach a specific weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: Not like a specific number, no. I don’t have some exact weight written down or anything.\n\nIt’s more… I get stuck on how I look right before work. Like I’ll be in the mirror checking if my waist looks tight enough, or if my face looks kind of sharp, not puffy. And if it doesn’t look right to me, it messes with my head going in. I feel less confident, like I don’t look as… ready to be in charge, I guess.\n\nWhich is weird, because with firefighting it’s not supposed to be about that. It’s supposed to be whether you can do the job, whether your gear’s right, whether you’re squared away. But somehow that “squared away” thing turns into me looking at my body too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to maintain a certain body shape or weight to be considered a good firefighter?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “need” exactly… like nobody at the station is telling me, “You have to look a certain way.” It’s not like that.\n\nBut in my head, yeah, kind of. Before work I’ll catch myself in the mirror, and I’m looking at my waist, my face, whether I look sharp enough or kind of soft or puffy. And if it doesn’t look right, it messes with me. I feel less confident walking in, like I’m not as command-ready somehow.\n\nWhich sounds stupid saying out loud, because being a good firefighter is supposed to be about doing the work, not your face in a mirror. But I guess I do connect it in my head more than I should.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
